| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-66376")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication CloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("Consent to application.","Add OAuth2PermissionGrant.","Add delegated permission grant.")
| project Timestamp, AccountObjectId, AccountDisplayName, ActivityType,
          ActivityObjects, IPAddress, UserAgent
// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d05

tags:

    - attack.persistence

    product: azure
    service: auditlogs

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.execution
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
    selection_args:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'
            - '-WindowStyle Hidden'
            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"

          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Glassworm Strikes Popular React Native Phone Number Packages
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Glassworm stage-3 persistence: schtasks UpdateApp + HKCU Run DPKCbbQ · [LLM] Glassworm side-staged Node.js runtime under %APPDATA%\_node_x86 / _node_x64 · Suspicious browser extension installation · Scheduled task created with suspicious image / encoded args · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Glassworm stage-2/stage-3 C2 callback to 45.32.150.251 or 217.69.3.152 · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs · Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process
Glassworm stage-2/stage-3 C2 callback to 45.32.150.251 or 217.69.3.152 AICommand & Control Medium
Detects outbound HTTP from any host to the two attacker-controlled IPs hosting the Glassworm stage-2 decryptor and stage-3 stealer C2 endpoints (/get_arhive_npm/, /wall). These IPs are unique to this campaign and any contact is a high-fidelity indicator that a developer or CI runner installed the backdoored react-native-country-select@0.3.91 or react-native-international-phone-number@0.11.8 package. Rationale: Both /16-unique IPs are explicitly named in the article as stage-2 fetch host (45.32.150.251) and stage-3 exfil endpoint (217.69.3.152:80/wall). URL fragments get_arhive_npm and 3e4Tg8V are unique slug patterns from the recovered payloads. Single hit = compromise.

Glassworm stage-3 persistence: schtasks UpdateApp + HKCU Run DPKCbbQ AIInstallation High
Detects the dual-persistence pattern dropped by the Glassworm stage-3 stealer: a scheduled task named 'UpdateApp' with /sc onstart /rl highest running a hidden PowerShell, and an HKCU Run key named 'DPKCbbQ'. Both indicators are hard-coded in the recovered payload and unlikely to collide with legitimate software. Rationale: The literal task name 'UpdateApp', Run value 'DPKCbbQ', /sc onstart with /rl highest, and hidden-bypass powershell launcher are all directly quoted from the recovered stage-3 script. They are arbitrary attacker-chosen strings and collide with no known legitimate software.
ATT&CKT1053.005 T1547.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Registry
let TaskPersistence = DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "schtasks.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "UpdateApp"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "onstart"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Bypass","-rl highest")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Indicator="schtasks_UpdateApp";
let RegPersistence = DeviceRegistryEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("RegistryValueSet","RegistryKeyCreated")
| where RegistryKey has @"\CurrentVersion\Run"
| where RegistryValueName =~ "DPKCbbQ"
   or RegistryValueData has @"powershell" and RegistryValueData has "WindowStyle Hidden" and RegistryValueData has "Bypass"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RegistryKey, RegistryValueName,
          RegistryValueData, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Indicator="HKCU_Run_DPKCbbQ";
union TaskPersistence, RegPersistence
| order by Timestamp desc
let TaskPersistence = SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\schtasks.exe"
| where CommandLine has "UpdateApp"
| where CommandLine has "onstart"
| where CommandLine has_any ("Bypass","highest")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName, Indicator="schtasks_UpdateApp";
let RegPersistence = ImRegistryEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where RegistryKey has @"\CurrentVersion\Run"
| where RegistryValue =~ "DPKCbbQ"
   or (RegistryValueData has "powershell" and RegistryValueData has "WindowStyle Hidden" and RegistryValueData has "Bypass")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, RegistryKey,
          RegistryValue, RegistryValueData,
          ActingProcessName, Indicator="HKCU_Run_DPKCbbQ";
union TaskPersistence, RegPersistence
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Glassworm side-staged Node.js runtime under %APPDATA%\_node_x86 / _node_x64 AIInstallation High
Detects the distinctive Glassworm stage-3 behaviour of downloading the full Node.js v22.9.0 Windows runtime ZIP from nodejs.org and unpacking it under %APPDATA%\_node_x86 or %APPDATA%\_node_x64. The stealer ships its own Node runtime so it can execute the payload on hosts without Node installed; these exact folder names are hard-coded. Rationale: The folder names _node_x86 and _node_x64 under %APPDATA% are literal strings in the recovered stage-3 JavaScript, as is the pinned Node.js v22.9.0 download URL. Legitimate Node installers do not place runtimes there — these are attacker-chosen sandbox staging directories.
ATT&CKT1105 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Web.Web
let SideStageFolders = dynamic([@"\AppData\Roaming\_node_x86\", @"\AppData\Roaming\_node_x64\"]);
let NodeZips = dynamic(["node-v22.9.0-win-x86.zip","node-v22.9.0-win-x64.zip"]);
let FileSig = DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has_any (SideStageFolders) or FileName in~ (NodeZips)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessAccountName, Indicator="appdata_node_runtime";
let NetSig = DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "nodejs.org/download/release/v22.9.0/node-v22.9.0-win"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, Indicator="nodejs_zip_download_nonbrowser";
union FileSig, NetSig
| order by Timestamp desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26") or SHA1 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26") or MD5 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26") or SHA1 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26") or MD5 in~ ("59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["59221aa9623d86c930357dba7e3f54138c7ccbd0daa9c483d766cd8ce1b6ad26"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

GlassWorm Mar 2026 wave — compromised npm/VS Code package artifacts on disk AIDelivery High
Detects installation of the four specific package versions named in the Aikido write-up (@aifabrix/miso-client 4.7.2, @iflow-mcp/watercrawl-watercrawl-mcp 1.3.0-1.3.4, quartz.quartz-markdown-editor 0.3.0) by alerting on file creation under their canonical node_modules / VS Code extension paths. Catches both interactive `npm install` on developer endpoints and CI/CD runners that pulled the poisoned versions. Rationale: Pivots on the four exact package/version strings called out in Aikido's article — @aifabrix/miso-client@4.7.2, @iflow-mcp/watercrawl-watercrawl-mcp@1.3.0-1.3.4, quartz.quartz-markdown-editor@0.3.0. Node package managers and VS Code persist these to canonical `node_modules\<scope>\<name>` and `.vscode\extensions\<id>-<ver>` paths regardless of how they were installed (npm/yarn/pnpm/CI runner), so a file-event match is invariant to invocation method. Both Windows backslash and Linux/macOS forward-slash variants are covered for cross-platform CI runners. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/glassworm-returns-unicode-attack-github-npm-vscode • https://thehackernews.com/2026/03/glassworm-supply-chain-attack-abuses-72.html • https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-glassworm-supply-chain-developer-toolchain/ • https://winbuzzer.com/2026/03/16/glassworm-invisible-unicode-supply-chain-attack-github-npm-vscode-xcxwbn/

GlassWorm invisible-Unicode decoder signature (variation-selector eval loader) in process cmdline AIExploitation High
Hunts for the unique GlassWorm JavaScript decoder pattern executing on the host — a loop over Unicode Variation Selectors (U+FE00–U+FE0F and U+E0100–U+E01EF) feeding `Buffer.from(...).toString('utf-8')` into `eval()`. The hex literals 0xFE00, 0xE0100 alongside `codePointAt` in the same node/PowerShell/cmd command line are not present in legitimate JavaScript and are diagnostic of this loader. Rationale: The decoder snippet quoted verbatim in the Aikido article (`w >= 0xFE00 && w <= 0xFE0F ? w - 0xFE00 : w >= 0xE0100 && w <= 0xE01EF ? w - 0xE0100 + 16 : null` followed by `eval(Buffer.from(s(\`\`)).toString('utf-8'))`) is a stable, unique fingerprint of the GlassWorm loader. Hex literals `0xFE00` and `0xE0100` are 6-character alphanumeric tokens — indexed by Defender/Sentinel `has`. We require all three discriminators (both ranges + the iterator API) so legitimate Unicode normalisation libraries (which usually reference only one VS range) won't false-positive. Corroborated against Cloud Security Alliance, Hacker News, and dev.to write-ups that all reproduce the same decoder shape. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/glassworm-returns-unicode-attack-github-npm-vscode • https://thehackernews.com/2026/03/glassworm-supply-chain-attack-abuses-72.html • https://labs.cloudsecurityalliance.org/research/csa-research-note-glassworm-supply-chain-developer-toolchain/ • https://dev.to/ohmygod/glassworm-how-invisible-unicode-characters-and-solana-are-powering-the-biggest-supply-chain-attack-4a4j • https://securityboulevard.com/2026/03/an-evolving-glassworm-malware-is-making-the-rounds-of-code-repositories/
ATT&CKT1027 T1140 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "0xFE00"
   and ProcessCommandLine has "0xE0100"
   and ProcessCommandLine has "codePointAt"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          Child = FileName,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "0xFE00"
   and TargetProcessCommandLine has "0xE0100"
   and TargetProcessCommandLine has "codePointAt"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine,
          TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"

          NewProcessName, CommandLine

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)

    category: process_creation

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'

            - '\mshta.exe'

            - '\wmic.exe'

DRILLAPP: Edge launched headless with media/security guardrails disabled AIInstallation High
Hunts the exact Microsoft Edge command-line fingerprint used by DRILLAPP to weaponise the browser as a backdoor — headless mode combined with --disable-web-security, --allow-file-access-from-files, --use-fake-ui-for-media-stream, --auto-select-screen-capture-source=true and --disable-user-media-security. This combination auto-grants camera/mic/screen capture and breaks the same-origin model, and is not used by any legitimate Edge automation in normal browsing. Rationale: These Edge flags are an exact quote from the article describing the DRILLAPP runtime — they auto-grant mic/camera/screen capture and disable the same-origin model. Combined with --headless they have no legitimate end-user browsing use; the only common benign caller is internal browser-automation rigs. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/007/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1123 T1125 T1113
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal QA / Selenium / Playwright automation rigs launching Edge headless with --disable-web-security for cross-origin test harnesses
  • DevOps screenshot / PDF-rendering services launching Edge headless with --no-sandbox in containers
  • Penetration-test or red-team tooling launching Edge with the same flags
  • Corporate accessibility-testing tools that need --use-fake-ui-for-media-stream
  • Edge WebDriver sessions triggered by Microsoft Intune / Endpoint Manager scripts
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chromedriver.exe","msedgedriver.exe","node.exe","python.exe","pytest.exe","selenium.exe")) | where not(InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\Jenkins\\","\\BuildAgent\\","\\AzureDevOps\\","\\GitHubActions\\"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): pull the full InitiatingProcessCommandLine and InitiatingProcessFolderPath — confirm whether the parent is explorer.exe / svchost.exe / a CPL or LNK handler (suspicious) versus a known automation binary (chromedriver.exe, node.exe under a CI agent path). Pull the spawning user — if it is an end-user account on a non-developer endpoint, escalate. Step 2 (contain): isolate the device via Defender MDE Live Response; kill the msedge.exe PID tree; block the parent binary's SHA256. Step 3 (hunt): in DeviceNetworkEvents pivot on the same msedge.exe ProcessId for outbound connections to pastefy.app, 80.89.224.13, 188.137.228.162, or any WebSocket to localhost:8000; in DeviceFileEvents look for HTML files written to %TEMP% in the 60s before the launch and for any .lnk drops in %APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "msedge.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "--headless"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--disable-web-security","--allow-file-access-from-files","--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream","--auto-select-screen-capture-source=true","--disable-user-media-security","--no-sandbox")
| extend FlagCount = countof(ProcessCommandLine, "--disable-web-security") + countof(ProcessCommandLine, "--allow-file-access-from-files") + countof(ProcessCommandLine, "--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream") + countof(ProcessCommandLine, "--auto-select-screen-capture-source") + countof(ProcessCommandLine, "--disable-user-media-security")
| where FlagCount >= 2
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ProcessCommandLine, FlagCount, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\msedge.exe"
| where CommandLine has "--headless"
| where CommandLine has_any ("--disable-web-security","--allow-file-access-from-files","--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream","--auto-select-screen-capture-source=true","--disable-user-media-security","--no-sandbox")
| extend FlagCount = countof(CommandLine, "--disable-web-security") + countof(CommandLine, "--allow-file-access-from-files") + countof(CommandLine, "--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream") + countof(CommandLine, "--auto-select-screen-capture-source") + countof(CommandLine, "--disable-user-media-security")
| where FlagCount >= 2
| where not(SubjectUserName endswith "$")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, FlagCount
title: DRILLAPP Edge Headless With Media And Security Guardrails Disabled
id: 3b8d6d31-2b6a-4d7a-9d62-c2c44f0a1a51
description: Detects Microsoft Edge launched headless with multiple flags that disable browser sandboxing, web-security and auto-grant camera/mic/screen capture, matching the DRILLAPP browser-backdoor command line.
references:
  - https://lab52.io/blog/drillapp-new-backdoor-targeting-ukrainian-entities-with-possible-links-to-laundry-bear/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/20
tags:
  - attack.t1059.007
  - attack.t1123
  - attack.t1125
  - attack.t1113

  category: process_creation
  product: windows

      - '--use-fake-ui-for-media-stream'
      - '--auto-select-screen-capture-source=true'

level: high

#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\msedge\.exe$/i

| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

DRILLAPP variant 2: Edge launched with --remote-debugging-port=9222 for CDP-based file download AICommand & Control High
Hunts the DRILLAPP variant-2 abuse of Chrome DevTools Protocol — msedge.exe spawned with --remote-debugging-port=9222 by a non-developer-tooling parent, which the article identifies as the only way the JavaScript backdoor can persuade the browser to download remote files (bypassing JavaScript's same-origin file-write restriction). Rationale: Article explicitly states variant 2 needs --remote-debugging-port enabled to use CDP and that port 9222 is the observed value (image at lab52.io shows '9222'). Outside of developer tooling and Selenium/Playwright/Puppeteer, end-user Edge processes never enable a debug port — the filter on dev-tooling parents drives FP rate near zero. Cross-checked against: • https://chromedevtools.github.io/devtools-protocol/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1105 T1218
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running Playwright / Puppeteer / Selenium against Edge with debug port for local UI tests
  • VS Code 'Debug Edge' launch configuration spawning Edge with the flag
  • Corporate UX automation suites (Applitools, BrowserStack local) opening Edge with CDP
  • Microsoft Intune mobile-application-management agents that drive Edge for compliance screenshots
  • Security researcher / red-team manually attaching to Edge with --remote-debugging-port=9222
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\Microsoft VS Code\\" | where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\Playwright\\" | where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\node_modules\\"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): inspect ParentProcessName and parent path — if parent is a CPL handler (rundll32.exe shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL ...), explorer.exe directly under a user, or any binary under %TEMP% / %APPDATA%, treat as DRILLAPP-likely. Pull the SHA256 of the parent and check against the article hashes. Step 2 (contain): isolate the host with Defender MDE; kill the msedge.exe PID tree and the parent process; capture the parent binary and any HTML/JS files written to %TEMP% in the prior 5 minutes. Step 3 (hunt): query DeviceFileEvents for files written by this msedge.exe ProcessId in the post-launch window (CDP downloads land via the browser, not the parent); pivot DeviceNetworkEvents to pastefy.app, 80.89.224.13, 188.137.228.162, and any localhost:8000 WebSocket from msedge.exe; sweep enterprise for other hosts running CPL files from %TEMP% / %APPDATA%.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "msedge.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "--remote-debugging-port"
| extend DebugPort = extract(@"--remote-debugging-port=(\d{2,5})", 1, ProcessCommandLine)
| where isnotempty(DebugPort)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chromedriver.exe","msedgedriver.exe","node.exe","python.exe","code.exe","devenv.exe","pytest.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ProcessCommandLine, DebugPort, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\msedge.exe"
| where CommandLine has "--remote-debugging-port"
| extend DebugPort = extract(@"--remote-debugging-port=(\d{2,5})", 1, CommandLine)
| where isnotempty(DebugPort)
| where not(ParentProcessName endswith "\\chromedriver.exe")
| where not(ParentProcessName endswith "\\msedgedriver.exe")
| where not(ParentProcessName endswith "\\node.exe")
| where not(ParentProcessName endswith "\\code.exe")
| where not(ParentProcessName endswith "\\devenv.exe")
| where not(SubjectUserName endswith "$")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, DebugPort

DRILLAPP variant 1 persistence: LNK file written to user Startup folder by non-Explorer process AIInstallation High
Hunts DRILLAPP variant 1's exact persistence trick — the loader copies all .lnk files into %APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup so the lure LNK re-fires on logon. Detection scopes to non-Explorer writers since legitimate pinned-startup creation almost always goes through explorer.exe or a known installer signed by a trusted publisher. Rationale: Article quotes the exact persistence path %APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup and behaviour (the executable copies all .lnk files there). Legitimate writers of that folder are nearly always explorer.exe (user drag), OneDrive.exe (sync), or installers — scoping by writer process keeps fidelity very high. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001/
ATT&CKT1547.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate third-party installer (e.g. Zoom, Slack, Spotify) that drops a Startup LNK during install — surfaces as msiexec.exe / vendor setup.exe parent
  • User manually creating a shortcut by right-click → Send To → Startup (parent should be explorer.exe and is filtered)
  • Group Policy startup-shortcut deployment via gpscript.exe
  • IT-managed onboarding script (BigFix, Tanium, SCCM) copying a corporate LNK into Startup
  • User profile migration tool (USMT loadstate.exe) restoring Startup shortcuts
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessVersionInfoCompanyName has_any ("Microsoft Corporation","Zoom Video Communications","Slack Technologies","Spotify AB","Adobe Systems","Google LLC"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): resolve the dropped .lnk target — use Sysmon EID 1 / DeviceProcessEvents to find what the shortcut points at (parsing the LNK requires retrieval, but a follow-on logon execution will show the spawned binary). Inspect the InitiatingProcessFileName / InitiatingProcessCommandLine — if it is a CPL handler, mshta, wscript or an unsigned binary under %TEMP%, escalate. Step 2 (contain): isolate the host, delete the Startup LNK (after capturing), and kill any spawned child processes; block the writer's SHA256 across the fleet. Step 3 (hunt): sweep DeviceFileEvents fleet-wide for other Startup LNK writes by the same SHA256; pivot DeviceNetworkEvents from the writer to pastefy.app and the article IPs (80.89.224.13, 188.137.228.162); search DeviceProcessEvents for msedge.exe headless launches in the next 24h on the same host.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup"
| where FileName endswith ".lnk"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("explorer.exe","onedrive.exe","msiexec.exe","setup.exe","trustedinstaller.exe","officeclicktorun.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
Event
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where Source == "Microsoft-Windows-Sysmon" and EventID == 11
| extend EventDataDyn = parse_xml(EventData)
| extend TargetFilename = tostring(EventDataDyn.DataItem.EventData.Data[5].['#text'])
| extend Image = tostring(EventDataDyn.DataItem.EventData.Data[4].['#text'])
| where TargetFilename has @"\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup"
| where TargetFilename endswith ".lnk"
| where not(Image endswith "\\explorer.exe")
| where not(Image endswith "\\OneDrive.exe")
| where not(Image endswith "\\msiexec.exe")
| where not(Image endswith "\\setup.exe")
| where not(Image endswith "\\TrustedInstaller.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Image, TargetFilename

DRILLAPP C2 staging: msedge.exe contacting pastefy.app AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts msedge.exe outbound HTTPS to pastefy.app — the article-named text-paste service DRILLAPP uses to host both the obfuscated JS backdoor and the WebSocket C2 URL retrieved at runtime. Browser → pastefy.app is unusual on a corporate endpoint and is the cleanest single-event pivot the article gives us. Rationale: pastefy.app is named twice in the article (payload hosting and WebSocket-URL dead-drop). The constraint that the requesting process is msedge.exe ties the hunt to the DRILLAPP browser-backdoor pattern rather than catching any user who paste-tests a snippet via a different browser. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1102/001/ • https://pastefy.app/
ATT&CKT1102.001 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer or sysadmin pasting / retrieving a snippet from pastefy.app via Edge for benign reasons
  • Security researcher manually fetching the article IOCs to study them
  • Microsoft Bing chat or Copilot session embedding a pastefy URL the user clicked
  • User following a pastefy.app link shared in Teams / Slack / email
  • Threat-intel analyst opening pastefy.app for a different (unrelated) snippet
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("developer","--devtools","about:blank")) | where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "--headless"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): pull DeviceNetworkEvents for the full URL path of the pastefy.app request — DRILLAPP-style requests will be GETs to /raw/<paste-id> followed by Edge fetching JS; user browsing will be the HTML pastefy.app landing pages. Correlate with DeviceProcessEvents for the msedge.exe PID — if the same PID was launched with --headless and the disabled-media flags, treat as confirmed. Step 2 (contain): isolate the host; block pastefy.app at the proxy/DNS sinkhole; capture the msedge.exe memory if possible. Step 3 (hunt): sweep DeviceProcessEvents for msedge.exe with --remote-debugging-port=9222 on the same host (variant 2 follow-on); query DeviceNetworkEvents for any subsequent traffic from msedge.exe to 80.89.224.13 / 188.137.228.162 / localhost:8000 or to a WebSocket URL retrieved from the pastefy paste; pivot to DeviceFileEvents for the LNK / CPL parent that spawned this Edge instance.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "msedge.exe"
| where RemoteUrl has "pastefy.app" or RemoteUrl endswith "pastefy.app"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessId
| order by Timestamp desc
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where Name has "pastefy.app"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, ResultCode
| union (
  CommonSecurityLog
  | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
  | where DestinationHostName has "pastefy.app" or RequestURL has "pastefy.app"
  | where RequestClientApplication has "Edg" or SourceUserName != ""
  | project TimeGenerated, Computer=SourceHostName, ClientIP=SourceIP, Name=DestinationHostName, QueryType="HTTP", ResultCode=tostring(EventOutcome)
)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

DRILLAPP variant 2 delivery: CPL file executed from user-writable folder spawning Edge AIDelivery High
Hunts DRILLAPP variant-2 delivery — rundll32.exe shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL <path>.cpl executing a .cpl from %TEMP% / %APPDATA% / Downloads, which in turn launches msedge.exe. CPL files are signed DLLs that double-click to execute, and the article explicitly states variant 2 abandoned LNK for CPL. Rationale: Article identifies variant 2's switch to CPL files (Windows Control Panel modules that internally are executable DLLs). The signature is rundll32.exe shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL <path>.cpl with the path under user-writable directories — the temporal join to msedge.exe spawn captures the article's full delivery → execution chain. Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1218/002/ • https://lolbas-project.github.io/lolbas/Binaries/Control/
ATT&CKT1218.002 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT admin manually opening a Control Panel applet from a network share or Downloads (uncommon but possible)
  • Software installer dropping a .cpl into %TEMP% before registering it (very rare modern installers)
  • Sysadmin troubleshooting a corrupt Control Panel item by re-running it manually
  • Vendor diagnostic utility (e.g. Intel / Nvidia driver helpers) that registers a .cpl from %APPDATA%
  • Penetration testing using LOLBAS Control.exe / rundll32 CPL hijack technique
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\Users\\","\\Temp\\") | where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","msiexec.exe","trustedinstaller.exe"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): retrieve the .cpl file and check its SHA256 against the article hashes (107b2badfc93fcdd3ffda7d3999477ced3f39f43f458dd0f6a424c9ab52681c3 and the other variant-2 IOCs); inspect the file with `Get-AuthenticodeSignature` — DRILLAPP CPLs were observed unsigned or with mismatched signatures. Confirm the msedge.exe child has DRILLAPP flags. Step 2 (contain): isolate the host, kill the rundll32+msedge.exe tree, block the CPL SHA256 enterprise-wide via Defender custom indicator. Step 3 (hunt): DeviceFileEvents — find how the .cpl arrived (email attachment, Edge download from short-link.net / iili.io, archive extraction); DeviceNetworkEvents from msedge.exe — sweep for pastefy.app / 80.89.224.13 / 188.137.228.162 / WebSocket to localhost:8000; cross-fleet hunt for the same SHA256.
let CplLaunch = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(14d)
    | where FileName =~ "rundll32.exe"
    | where ProcessCommandLine has "shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL"
    | where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\.cpl"
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any (@"\AppData\Local\Temp\", @"\AppData\Roaming\", @"\Downloads\", @"\Users\Public\")
    | project CplTime=Timestamp, DeviceId, RundllPid=ProcessId, CplCmd=ProcessCommandLine, AccountName, DeviceName;
let EdgeSpawn = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(14d)
    | where FileName =~ "msedge.exe"
    | project EdgeTime=Timestamp, DeviceId, ParentPid=InitiatingProcessId, EdgeCmd=ProcessCommandLine;
CplLaunch
| join kind=inner EdgeSpawn on DeviceId, $left.RundllPid == $right.ParentPid
| where EdgeTime between (CplTime .. CplTime + 5m)
| project CplTime, EdgeTime, DeviceName, AccountName, CplCmd, EdgeCmd
let CplLaunch = SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where NewProcessName endswith "\\rundll32.exe"
    | where CommandLine has "shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL"
    | where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\.cpl"
    | where CommandLine has_any (@"\AppData\Local\Temp\", @"\AppData\Roaming\", @"\Downloads\", @"\Users\Public\")
    | project CplTime=TimeGenerated, Computer, RundllPid=tostring(NewProcessId), CplCmd=CommandLine, SubjectUserName;
let EdgeSpawn = SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where NewProcessName endswith "\\msedge.exe"
    | project EdgeTime=TimeGenerated, Computer, ParentPid=tostring(ProcessId), EdgeCmd=CommandLine;
CplLaunch
| join kind=inner EdgeSpawn on Computer, $left.RundllPid == $right.ParentPid
| where EdgeTime between (CplTime .. CplTime + 5m)
| project CplTime, EdgeTime, Computer, SubjectUserName, CplCmd, EdgeCmd
source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.security) (@EventID:1 OR @EventID:4688) @Image:(*\rundll32.exe OR *\RUNDLL32.EXE) @CommandLine:*shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL* @CommandLine:*.cpl* (@CommandLine:*\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\* OR @CommandLine:*\\AppData\\Roaming\\* OR @CommandLine:*\\Downloads\\* OR @CommandLine:*\\Users\\Public\\*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\rundll32\.exe$/i
| regex("shell32\\.dll,Control_RunDLL", field=CommandLine)
| regex("\\.cpl(\\s|$|\")", field=CommandLine)
| regex("\\\\(AppData\\\\(Local|Roaming)\\\\Temp\\\\|\\\\Downloads\\\\|\\\\Users\\\\Public\\\\)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as cmdline values(Processes.parent_process_name) as parent values(Processes.user) as user from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" Processes.process="*shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL*" (Processes.process="*\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\AppData\\Roaming\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\Downloads\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\Users\\Public\\*") Processes.process="*.cpl*" by host Processes.process_name Processes.parent_process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Processes.process) as cmdline values(Processes.parent_process_name) as parent values(Processes.user) as user from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" Processes.process="*shell32.dll,Control_RunDLL*" (Processes.process="*\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\AppData\\Roaming\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\Downloads\\*" OR Processes.process="*\\Users\\Public\\*") Processes.process="*.cpl*" by host Processes.process_name Processes.parent_process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
DRILLAPP C2: msedge.exe egress to known DRILLAPP IPs or WebSocket to localhost:8000 AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts post-installation C2 from the DRILLAPP backdoor — msedge.exe reaching either of the two corroborated DRILLAPP IPs (80.89.224.13, 188.137.228.162) on any port, or any process opening a WebSocket to localhost:8000 (the article-named debugging-trace default the operators planned to swap for a real IP). Rationale: Article supplies the C2 IPs as pre-extracted IOCs and explicitly names localhost:8000 as the default WebSocket destination DRILLAPP falls back to when the pastefy.app URL is unavailable (operators stated this is intended to be replaced with a real IP in later builds — but until then it is a unique fingerprint of an in-the-wild backdoor that didn't get its C2 URL). Cross-checked against: • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/001/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1090
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer running a local web service on port 8000 (Python http.server, Django runserver, Node) and visiting it in Edge — the loopback leg only
  • Internal infrastructure that happens to reuse 80.89.224.13 or 188.137.228.162 (verify against ASN / abuse.ch before alerting)
  • Microsoft Edge DevTools auto-connecting to a local debugger on 8000
  • Browser extension testing harness opening a WebSocket to localhost:8000
  • Threat-intel analyst manually fetching the IOCs in an isolated VM that erroneously routes through corporate egress
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--devtools","--inspect","--auto-open-devtools-for-tabs"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): for IP hits — verify the destination is still attributable to DRILLAPP via abuse.ch / VirusTotal passive DNS; pull the InitiatingProcessFileName + command line, confirm msedge.exe with DRILLAPP-style flags. For localhost:8000 — confirm the msedge.exe was launched with --headless and the disabled-media flags (otherwise it's likely a developer). Step 2 (contain): isolate the host; block 80.89.224.13 and 188.137.228.162 at the firewall / proxy; kill the msedge.exe PID tree. Step 3 (hunt): sweep DeviceNetworkEvents fleet-wide for the same IPs over 30 days; pivot to the spawning CPL / LNK via DeviceProcessEvents and DeviceFileEvents; check DeviceFileEvents for any files msedge.exe wrote to %USERPROFILE%\Downloads in the surrounding 1h window (variant 2 CDP-driven downloads).
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (RemoteIP in ("80.89.224.13","188.137.228.162"))
    or (InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "msedge.exe" and RemoteIP in ("127.0.0.1","::1") and RemotePort == 8000)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, Protocol
| order by Timestamp desc
let DrillappIPs = dynamic(["80.89.224.13","188.137.228.162"]);
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationIP in (DrillappIPs)
   or (SourceProcessName has "msedge.exe" and DestinationIP in ("127.0.0.1","::1") and DestinationPort == 8000)
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, SourceHostName, SourceIP, SourceUserName, SourceProcessName, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestURL, ApplicationProtocol
| union (
  Event
  | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
  | where Source == "Microsoft-Windows-Sysmon" and EventID == 3
  | extend EventDataDyn = parse_xml(EventData)
  | extend DestinationIp = tostring(EventDataDyn.DataItem.EventData.Data[14].['#text'])
  | extend DestinationPort = toint(EventDataDyn.DataItem.EventData.Data[15].['#text'])
  | extend Image = tostring(EventDataDyn.DataItem.EventData.Data[4].['#text'])
  | where DestinationIp in (DrillappIPs)
     or (Image endswith "\\msedge.exe" and DestinationIp in ("127.0.0.1","::1") and DestinationPort == 8000)
  | project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor="Sysmon", SourceHostName=Computer, SourceIP="", SourceUserName="", SourceProcessName=Image, DestinationIP=DestinationIp, DestinationPort, RequestURL="", ApplicationProtocol=""
)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(-enc|encodedcommand|frombase64string|-nop|-w\s+hidden|invoke-expression|iex\s*\(|downloadstring|net\.webclient)"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.execution
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
    selection_args:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'
            - '-WindowStyle Hidden'
            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*-enc *" OR Processes.process="*EncodedCommand*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

let BadHashes = dynamic(["5b978cdc46afa28d83e532cd19622d9097bebedf87efc4c87bd35d8ffad9e672", "6178b1af51057c0bac75a842afff500a8fa3ed957d79a712a6ef089bec7e7a8b", "ac60eefc2607216f8126c0b22b6243f3862ef2bb265c585deee0d00a20a436b3", "e20831cecd763d0dc91fb39f3bd61d17002608c5a40a6cf0bd16111f4e50d341", "ee90b01b16099e0bb23d4653607a3a559590fc8d0c43120b8456fb1860d2e630", "32973ef02e10a585a4a0196b013265e29fc57d8e1c50752f7b39e43b9f388715", "107b2badfc93fcdd3ffda7d3999477ced3f39f43f458dd0f6a424c9ab52681c3", "a545908c931ec47884b5ccfb1f112435f5d0cdac140e664673672c9df9016672", "352f34ea5cc40e2b3ec056ae60fa19a368dbd42503ef225cb1ca57956eb05e81", "c6905bae088982a2b234451b45db742098f2e2ab4fd6ca62c8f4e801160552aa", "993d55f60414bf2092f421c3d0ac6af1897a21cc4ea260ae8e610a402bf4c81c", "ccb7d999ee4d979e175b8c87e09ccda0cbc93b6140471283e3a1f1f9da33759d", "51e86408904c0ca3778361cde746783a0f2b9fd2a6782aa7e062aa597151876e", "fb16933b09a4fcca5beff93da05566e924017fb534a2f45caf57b57a633f43a6", "2b5d8f8db5fd38ae1c34807dcba35b057cffa61eb14ba3b558f82eb630480c3f", "eb9c1649e01db6a9a94d5d50373e54865d672b14ad6f221c98047c562d3cc0f3", "8c6ea44ce7f4ed4e4e7e19e11b3b345d58785c93b33aa795ddd1b0d753236b05", "66a7828bc8c6c783b2ffa3c906d53f6dae1bbddc019283cc369d7d73247c5181", "886df55794cbca146de96dcc626471b3c097a5c20ba488033b24f4347aa20a14", "9367f4b4d2775ff47279d143dd9a0ef544ddff81946aab33da9350a49f14e1e1", "b891fa118db5190f07b18be46eb9bc10677f9afab1406a7d52ce587522ab3d28", "bad7c6f6ca25363a02eaceb3ed1e378218dc4a246a63d723cfcc5feee3af5056", "21fefc3913d3d2dfde7f0dff54800ca7512eb5df9513b1a457a2af25fdd51b26", "801c47550799831bfb1ac6c5c3fd698be95da19fc85bd65f5d8639f26244d2a9", "6fea579685d2433cedb1c32ef704575dcbc1d0a623769e824023ffccd0dedaae", "76eb713e38f145ee68b89f2febd8f9a28bbb2b464da61cb029d84433a0b2c746"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    Replace `__HASH_LIST__` placeholder with article-specific SHA256
    values. Use a watchlist or external indicator file for management
    at scale.

    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/

date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.t1027
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Hashes|contains:
            - 'SHA256=__HASH_PLACEHOLDER_REPLACE_ME__'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - None on a true-positive hash match
level: critical

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-3910 — Google Chromium V8 Improper Restriction of Operations Within the Bounds of a Memory Buffer Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-03-13 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Google Chromium V8 contains an improper restriction of operations within the bounds of a memory buffer vulnerability that could allow a remote attacker to execute arbitrary code inside a sandbox via a crafted HTML page. This vulnerability could affect multiple web browsers that utilize Chromium, including, but not limited to, Google Chrome, Microsoft Edge, and Opera. Vendor: Google, Product: Chromium V8. Federal patch due: 2026-03-27.

The cybersecurity implications of the war in the Middle East extend far beyond the region. Here’s where to focus your defenses.

MuddyWater SimpleHelp RMM client spawning shell or recon LOLBin AIInstallation High
Detects the MuddyWater (Iran/MOIS) hands-on-keyboard pattern explicitly called out by ESET: after gaining access via an MSP's exposed SimpleHelp Remote Access server (also tied to CVE-2024-57727 in CISA AA25-163A), operators drive the SimpleHelp client process to spawn interactive shells, scripting hosts, and built-in Windows recon utilities. Legitimate MSP automation almost never spawns whoami / nltest / quser from the SimpleHelp client. Rationale: Article names MuddyWater's SimpleHelp abuse at MSPs as the marquee Iran-aligned RMM TTP. Pairing the parent SimpleHelp binaries with the LOLBin/shell child set is the operator's hands-on pattern (vs benign MSP scripted automation, which usually invokes msiexec / specific custom installers). Cross-checked against CISA AA25-163A SimpleHelp ransomware advisory and HawkEye/Symantec MuddyWater write-ups listing SimpleHelp alongside Atera/ScreenConnect/N-able as the group's preferred RMM stack. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa25-163a • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/06/12/cisa-releases-cybersecurity-advisory-simplehelp-rmm-vulnerability • https://hawk-eye.io/wp-content/advisories/muddywater-threat-advisory.html • https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0069/
ATT&CKT1219 T1059.001 T1059.003 T1087 T1199
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("SimpleHelpCustomer.exe","Remote Access.exe","SimpleService.exe","JWrapper-Remote Access.exe")
   or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\JWrapper-Remote Access\"
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","net.exe","net1.exe","whoami.exe","nltest.exe","quser.exe","systeminfo.exe","ipconfig.exe","tasklist.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd   = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage  = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd    = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\\SimpleHelpCustomer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\\Remote Access.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\\SimpleService.exe"
   or ParentProcessName has @"\JWrapper-Remote Access\"
| where NewProcessName has_any (@"\cmd.exe", @"\powershell.exe", @"\pwsh.exe", @"\wscript.exe", @"\cscript.exe", @"\mshta.exe", @"\rundll32.exe", @"\regsvr32.exe", @"\bitsadmin.exe", @"\certutil.exe", @"\net.exe", @"\net1.exe", @"\whoami.exe", @"\nltest.exe", @"\quser.exe", @"\systeminfo.exe", @"\ipconfig.exe", @"\tasklist.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, TokenElevationType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Iran-aligned MFA push-bombing followed by new auth method registered (AA24-290A) AIActions on Objectives High
Implements the exact persistence chain CISA/FBI/NSA AA24-290A documents and the article calls out: Iran-nexus operators flood a user with MFA prompts (push-bombing) until one is approved, then immediately enrol their own authenticator/phone against the victim account to lock in access. Correlates a burst of MFA-related Entra sign-in failures with a fresh security-info registration on the same UPN within 2 hours. Rationale: Article explicitly cites the joint CISA/FBI/NSA October 2024 advisory describing Iranian actors using password spraying + MFA push-bombing and then modifying MFA registrations for persistence. The detection is the literal sequence: ≥5 MFA-friction Entra failures (codes 500121/50074/50076/50097/50158 are the documented push-bombing footprint) followed within 2h by a security-info / authentication-method registration on the same UPN. Cross-checked against CISA advisory AA24-290A and Picus/AttackIQ technical breakdowns. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa24-290a • https://media.defense.gov/2024/Oct/16/2003565317/-1/-1/0/CSA-IRAN-CYBER-BRUTE-FORCE-CRITICAL-INFRASTRUCTURE-ORGS.PDF • https://www.picussecurity.com/resource/blog/cisa-alert-aa24-290a-iranian-cyber-actors-brute-force-and-credential-access-attacks • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1621/
ATT&CKT1621 T1110.003 T1556.006 T1098.005
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication Change.Account_Management
let LookbackHours = 24h;
let WindowMin = 120;
let BombingThreshold = 5;
// MFA-friction failure codes per Entra docs
let MfaFailCodes = dynamic([500121, 50074, 50076, 50097, 50158]);
let Bombed = AADSignInEventsBeta
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackHours)
    | where ErrorCode in (MfaFailCodes)
    | summarize FailCount = count(),
                FirstFail = min(Timestamp),
                LastFail  = max(Timestamp),
                SourceIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 25),
                SourceCountries = make_set(Country, 10)
                by AccountUpn, AccountObjectId
    | where FailCount >= BombingThreshold;
let MFAReg = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackHours)
    | where ApplicationId == 11161  // Office 365 / Azure AD audit feed
    | where ActionType has_any ("User registered security info",
                                "Admin registered security info",
                                "User registered all required security info",
                                "User started security info registration",
                                "Update user")
    | extend TargetUpn = tostring(parse_json(tostring(ActivityObjects))[0].name)
    | where isnotempty(TargetUpn)
    | project RegTime = Timestamp, TargetUpn, ActionType, RegIP = IPAddress,
              ActorDisplayName = AccountDisplayName;
Bombed
| join kind=inner MFAReg on $left.AccountUpn == $right.TargetUpn
| where RegTime between (LastFail .. LastFail + WindowMin * 1m)
| project AccountUpn, FailCount, FirstFail, LastFail, RegTime,
          MinutesAfterBombing = datetime_diff('minute', RegTime, LastFail),
          ActionType, RegIP, SourceIPs, SourceCountries
| order by RegTime desc
let LookbackHours = 24h;
let WindowMin = 120;
let BombingThreshold = 5;
// MFA-friction ResultType codes per Entra docs (push-bombing footprint)
let MfaFailCodes = dynamic([500121, 50074, 50076, 50097, 50158]);
let Bombed = SigninLogs
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackHours)
    | where ResultType in (MfaFailCodes)
    | extend Country = tostring(parse_json(tostring(LocationDetails)).countryOrRegion)
    | summarize FailCount = count(),
                FirstFail = min(TimeGenerated),
                LastFail  = max(TimeGenerated),
                SourceIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 25),
                SourceCountries = make_set(Country, 10)
                by UserPrincipalName, UserId
    | where FailCount >= BombingThreshold;
let MFAReg = AuditLogs
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackHours)
    | where LoggedByService == "Authentication Methods"
       or OperationName has_any ("security info",
                                  "User registered",
                                  "Admin registered",
                                  "Add user authentication method",
                                  "Update user authentication method",
                                  "Register device")
    | mv-expand TargetResources
    | extend TargetUpn = tostring(TargetResources.userPrincipalName)
    | extend Initiator = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName),
             InitiatorIP = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.ipAddress)
    | where isnotempty(TargetUpn)
    | project RegTime = TimeGenerated, TargetUpn, OperationName, Result, Initiator, InitiatorIP;
Bombed
| join kind=inner MFAReg on $left.UserPrincipalName == $right.TargetUpn
| where RegTime between (LastFail .. LastFail + WindowMin * 1m)
| project UserPrincipalName, FailCount, FirstFail, LastFail, RegTime,
          MinutesAfterBombing = datetime_diff('minute', RegTime, LastFail),
          OperationName, Result, Initiator, InitiatorIP, SourceIPs, SourceCountries
| order by RegTime desc
CLOUD

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07

description: |

    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/

author: Clankerusecase

    - attack.t1059

    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'

            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'

            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase

            - 'wget '

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200

          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
